represents the observer’s proper time. In order to guarantee that each signal sent by the emitter is received by the observer during the given time interval, one needs to introduce a geometrical object,
\vec{V}
, which records the variation of the null geodesic
\eta
connecting the worldlines of the source and the observer as time passes (See Fig
2
.).

Figure 2
:
The observation time vector
\vec{V}
records the variation of null geodesics as the emitter and the observer propagate on their own worldlines. It interpolates between the endpoint values
\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}
and
(1+z)^{-1}\,\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}
along a null geodesic connecting the worldlines of the observer
\mathscr{O}
and the emitter
\mathscr{E}
. Vectors
\vec{a}_{\mathscr{O}}
and
\vec{a}_{\mathscr{E}}
denote momentary accelerations of the observer and the emitter respectively at the moments of observation at
p_{\mathscr{O}}
and of emission at
p_{\mathscr{E}}
.

This object was first introduced in
[
78
]
and referred to as
infinitesimal message
. Later, it was further developed in
[
27
,
52
]
where it was referred to as
observation time vector
.

The vector
\vec{V}
has the following properties.

(i)

It keeps track of the deviation of
\eta
corresponding to the time interval of the observation. Therefore, it satisfies the geodesic deviation equation. In this work, we are concerned with the first order part of the dynamics. Then we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{l}}\nabla_{\vec{l}}V^{\alpha}=\mathchoice{R^{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\vec{l}}{\,\vec{l}}{\vec{V}}}}{R^{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\vec{l}}{\,\vec{l}}{\vec{V}}}}{R^{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\vec{l}}{\,\vec{l}}{\vec{V}}}}{R^{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70485pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\vec{l}}{\,\vec{l}}{\vec{V}}}}\,.

(111)

(ii)

The vector
\vec{V}
is necessarily a solution to the deviation of only the null geodesics. Hence,

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{l},\nabla_{\vec{l}}\vec{V}\,\rangle=0.

(112)

(iii)

The boundary conditions of
\vec{V}
are given by

\displaystyle\vec{V}\left(\lambda_{\mathscr{O}}\right)=\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}},\qquad\vec{V}\left(\lambda_{\mathscr{E}}\right)=\left(1+z\right)^{-1}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}},

(113)

where
\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}
is the 4–velocity of the observer,
\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}
is the one of the emitter and
z
is the redshift as defined in eq. (
104
).
Note that those boundary conditions follow from the fact that the null geodesic
\eta
intersects the worldlines of the emitter and the observer at the end points, i.e.,
\eta\left(\lambda_{\mathscr{O}}\right)=\gamma_{\mathscr{O}}\left(\tau^{\prime}\right)
and
\eta\left(\lambda_{\mathscr{E}}\right)=\gamma_{\mathscr{E}}\left(\tau\right)
. The need for the
(1+z)^{-1}
prefactor in front of
\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}
, on the other hand, follows from the null geodesic condition (
112
), see
[
27
,
52
]
for details. Physically, this pre-factor corresponds to the time dilation effect as the observer registers the emitter’s proper time slowed down by the factor
\left(1+z\right)^{-1}
. Note that this fact was verified experimentally by making use of the Type–Ia supernovae data before
[
79
]
.

It is then obvious that the vector
\vec{V}
evaluated at
p_{\mathscr{O}}
and at
p_{\mathscr{E}}
indeed defines a bi–local variation vector which causes the drift effects in general. Vector
\vec{V}
depends on both the 4–velocity of the source and of the observer. It tells us that the endpoint,
p_{\mathscr{O}}\,
, of the null geodesic at the observer is varied by
\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{O}}=\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}
and the other endpoint is varied by
\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{E}}=\left(1+z\right)^{-1}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}
at
p_{\mathscr{E}}\,
.
Those variations correspond to the changes that occur due to the kinematics of the observer and the emitter. Obviously, such a bi–local variation of endpoints is defined through
\bm{T}
of eq. (
103
).
We may therefore think of
\vec{V}
as the extension of
\bm{T}
to the full connecting null geodesic. It follows that we can obtain the variations of
\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}
and
\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}
under
\vec{V}
from
\bm{U}
. We use this fact in the derivation of the drifts below.

Redshift drift

The redshift drift is the change in the redshift recording of an object on the sky during a continuous observation. Another way of putting this is that it is an observable corresponding to the relative total change in the light signal caused by
\vec{V}
at both ends and measured by an observer with 4–velocity
\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}
and an emitter with 4–velocity
\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}
. Then, the endpoint variation is indeed equal to
\bm{T}
from eq. (
103
).

Let us now recall the covariant, bi–local variation operator,
\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{X}}
, defined in eqs. (
16
) and (
II.2
) respectively acting on bi–local vectors and generic bi–local tensors.

We may apply this operator to the logarithmic redshift,
\ln\left(1+z\right)
, in order to see the effect of bi–local variation caused by
\bm{T}
. This variation is obviously equal to the derivative of the logarithmic redshift with respect to the observer’s proper time
\tau^{\prime}
. We begin by substituting
\bm{T}
for the 8–dimensional generic variation vector
\bm{X}
in eq. (
II.2
). Then we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau^{\prime}}\ln(1+z)\equiv{\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{T}}}\ln\left(1+z\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\langle\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}},\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle}\Bigg(\Bigg.\langle\,{\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{T}}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}},\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle+\langle\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}},{\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{T}}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle\Bigg.\Bigg)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\langle\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}},\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle}\Bigg(\Bigg.\langle\,{\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{T}}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}},\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle+\langle\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}},{\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{T}}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle\Bigg.\Bigg).

Here, we have

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{T}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}\\
\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{T}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\Delta{\vec{l}}_{\mathscr{O}}\\
\Delta{\vec{l}}_{\mathscr{E}}\end{bmatrix},

(114)

giving the the covariant variations of the null tangent vector at both ends, under the assumption that

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{O}}\\
\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{E}}\end{bmatrix}={\bm{T}}.

(115)

Then, once we use (i) eqs. (
II.4
) and (
78
) to identify the sub–blocks of the matrix
\mathbf{U}
and (ii) the components of
\bm{T}=\left[\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\qquad(1+z)^{-1}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}\right]^{\intercal}
at both ends, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau^{\prime}}\ln\left(1+z\right)=\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\left(\frac{\langle\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}},\vec{a}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle}{(1+z)^{2}}-\langle\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}},\vec{a}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle-\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{T}},{\bm{T}}\right)\right),

where
\vec{a}_{\mathscr{E}}={u}_{\mathscr{E}}^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}
and
\vec{a}_{\mathscr{O}}={u}_{\mathscr{O}}^{\mu^{\prime}}\nabla_{\mu^{\prime}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}
are the coordinate accelerations of the emitter and the observer respectively. The redshift drift in eq. (
IV.2
) involves the line of sight Doppler effect through the acceleration differences of the emitter and the observer, in addition to the effect of geometry which manifests itself in
\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{T}},{\bm{T}}\right)
term, i.e., through the integrated components of the Riemann tensor along the null geodesic.

We finally note that it is also possible to express eq. (
IV.2
) with the help of the position
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}
and the viewing direction
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}
as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau^{\prime}}\ln\left(1+z\right)=\frac{\langle\,\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}},\vec{a}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle}{1+z}-\langle\,\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}},\vec{a}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle-\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\,\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{T}},{\bm{T}}\right),

through eqs. (
107
) and (
109
).

Position drift

The position drift
{{\cal D}}_{\tau^{\prime}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}

is defined as the change of the transverse components of the position vector
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}
given in eq. (
107
) with respect to the proper clocks of the observer and the emitter. The variation of the position is defined with respect to the Fermi–Walker transported spatial frame
[
27
,
52
]
. This variation can be expressed as the Fermi–Walker derivative
\cal D
of
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}

with respect to the observer’s proper time
\tau^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle{\cal D}_{\tau^{\prime}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}\equiv\frac{\cal D}{d\tau^{\prime}}\,\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}}\,\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}-\langle\,\vec{a}_{\mathscr{O}},\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle\,\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}+\langle\,\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}},\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle\,\vec{a}_{\mathscr{O}}.

(118)

The Fermi–Walker derivative is necessary in this context, because unlike the covariant derivative, it respects the decomposition into spatial vectors and
\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}
for accelerating observers as well
[
27
,
52
]
. Note that the last term in eq. (
118
) vanishes, so only the first two contribute.

It is a spatial and transverse vector, i.e.

\displaystyle\langle\,{\cal D}_{\tau^{\prime}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}},\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle=\langle\,{\cal D}_{\tau^{\prime}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}},\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle=0,

(119)

and therefore has only transverse components, related to the rate of change of the celestial coordinates of the source. In an adapted tetrad we obviously have

\displaystyle\langle\,{\cal D}_{\tau^{\prime}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}},\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}\,\rangle=\langle\,\nabla_{\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}},\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}\,\rangle,

(120)

i.e., the second term in (
118
) does not contribute and it is enough to calculate the transverse components of the covariant derivative of the position vector. Thus, the
position drift is similar to the redshift drift, with an additional requirement that only the observables projected on the observer’s transverse screen are of interest. Then, it can be calculated using a similar variation under
\bm{T}
:

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{T}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{T}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{T}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle-\frac{{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{T}}\,\omega_{\mathscr{O}}}{{\omega_{\mathscr{O}}}^{2}}\,\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle.

The last term vanishes because
\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}
is a transverse vector. The first term, just like in the redshift drift case, is simply equal to the observer’s 4-acceleration
\vec{a}_{\mathscr{O}}
.
Substitution of the covariant variation of the null tangent vector at the observer,
{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{T}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}=\Delta\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}
, in terms of the sub–blocks of the matrix
\mathbf{U}
and the components of
\bm{T}
gives

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},{\cal D}_{\tau^{\prime}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},\vec{a}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle+\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}{\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{T}},{\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}}\right)}.

The position drift in eq. (
IV.2
) involves an acceleration term which represents the non–gravitational aberration drift, i.e., the apparent position change of every source due to the gradual change of the frame caused by any non–gravitational acceleration. The effects of spacetime geometry are represented though
\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{T}},{\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}}\right)
, obtained by integrating the relevant components of the Riemann tensor.

Viewing direction drift

Let us recall that the viewing direction vector,
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}
, defined on the emitter’s side is essentially the analogue of the position vector,
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}
, defined on the observer’s side. Thus, we follow a similar method to derive the drift effect of the viewing direction vector. Namely, by using the definition of the viewing direction vector as in eq. (
109
) we identify how it changes with respect to the timelike variations at the both ends. The result is again projected on a local screen belonging to the emitter, providing this way the two non–vanishing, transverse components of the Fermi–Walker derivative of
\vec{r}_{\,\mathscr{E}}
along the source’s worldline. The construction is therefore in full analogy with the position drift. We may then derive the counterpart of eq. (
) as

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{A},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{T}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{A},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{T}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{A},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{T}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle-\frac{{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{T}}\,\omega_{\mathscr{E}}}{{\omega_{\mathscr{E}}}^{2}}\,\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{A},\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle.

The last term vanishes as before. For the first term in the above, we have
{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{T}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}=\left(1+z\right)^{-1}\,\nabla_{\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}=(1+z)^{-1}\vec{a}_{\mathscr{E}}
. We can also substitute

{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{T}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}=\Delta\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}
and express it via the sub–blocks of
\mathbf{U}
, to obtain the analogue of eq. (
IV.2
):

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{A},{\cal D}_{\tau^{\prime}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{A},\vec{a}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle}{1+z}-\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}{\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{T}},{\bm{E}_{A}^{\mathscr{E}}}\right)}.

As for the position drift, the operational definition of the viewing direction drift involves the Fermi–Walker transport of
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}
along the source’s timelike worldline. However, this does not affect the results as we are considering only the transverse components. We should also mention that the derivatives are taken again with respect to the
observer’s
proper time, as this seems more reasonable from the observational point of view. If one wants to obtain the drift of
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}
with respect to the emitter’s proper time, then one can adjust the result though the relationship
d\tau=\left(1+z\right)d\tau^{\prime}
.

IV.2.2
Effects of transverse variations

Until now we considered timelike bi–local variations defined through the 4–velocities of the observer and the emitter. Those resulted in drift effects. In this section, we define variational observables which are sourced by spacelike variations transverse to the null propagation direction.

The derivation process of those effects of the transverse variations is similar to the ones of drift effects. However, the variation bi–vector is different this time. Namely, instead of varying both ends with the bi–local vector
\bm{T}
, we consider variations of the form

\displaystyle\bm{X}=\delta x^{A^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}+\delta x^{A}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\bm{E}_{A}^{\mathscr{E}},

(125)

where
\delta x^{A^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}
and
\delta x^{A}_{\mathscr{E}}
represent the arbitrary values of the transverse variations at
p_{\mathscr{O}}\,
and at
p_{\mathscr{E}}\,
respectively. The bi–local vectors
\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}
and
\bm{E}_{A}^{\mathscr{E}}
are the permissible bi–local tetrad variations given in eq. (
103
).

In the calculations, we consider the position displacements of the emitter and the observer in tandem as in eq. (
125
). In the discussion of the physical interpretation of the results, on the other hand, we distinguish those displacements for a given scenario. The two physical setups we consider are presented in Fig. (
3(a)
) and (
3(b)
), respectively.

(a)

Transverse variations of the emission point by
\delta x_{\mathscr{E}}^{A}
. Those position variations lead to the variation of the null tangents at both endpoints. This results in the variation of the redshift, the apparent position and the viewing direction. In all cases the signal is received at the same position and at the same time, corresponding to the spacetime event
p_{\mathscr{O}}
. The time dimension is suppressed, while both transverse directions are present.

(b)

Transverse variations of the observation point
\delta x_{\mathscr{O}}^{A^{\prime}}
that lead to the variations of the null tangents. All observations correspond to the same emission point at the same emission moment, represented by the event
p_{\mathscr{E}}
. Time dimension is again suppressed, while both transverse directions are present.

Figure 3
:
Comparison of the transverse variations of the emitter and the observer.

Redshift under transverse variations

In this part, we investigate the variation of the redshift with respect to spatial variations of the emitter and the observer on the transverse directions. Here, we consider the changes in the logarithmic redshift again. We have a similar variational approach as in the drift case,

\displaystyle{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\ln\left(1+z\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\langle\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}},\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle}\Bigg(\Bigg.\langle\,{\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{X}}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}},\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle+\langle\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}},{\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{X}}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle\Bigg.\Bigg)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\langle\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}},\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle}\Bigg(\Bigg.\langle\,{\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{X}}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}},\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle+\langle\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}},{\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{X}}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle\Bigg.\Bigg).

with

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{X}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}\\
\bm{\nabla}_{\bm{X}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\Delta{\vec{l}}_{\mathscr{O}}\\
\Delta{\vec{l}}_{\mathscr{E}}\end{bmatrix},

(126)

giving the the covariant variations of the null tangent vector at both ends. In order to investigate the effect of the transverse variations on the redshift, however, we take the bi–local variation vector to be in the form in eq. (
125
) this time, i.e.,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{O}}\\
\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{E}}\end{bmatrix}=\delta x^{A^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}\begin{bmatrix}{\vec{e}}{{}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}}\\
0\end{bmatrix}+\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
{\vec{e}}{{}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{B}},\end{bmatrix}.

(127)

This allows us to consider only the spatial, transverse variations. Then, we obtain

\displaystyle{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\ln\left(1+z\right)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}\langle\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}},\nabla_{B}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle-\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\delta x^{A^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}\langle\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}},\nabla_{A^{\prime}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle-\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}{\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B}^{\mathscr{E}}},{\bm{T}}\right)}-\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\delta x^{A^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}{\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}},{\bm{T}}\right)}.

(128)

For the sake of simplicity we assume that the displaced source and observer are comoving with the original ones, i.e.
{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}=0={\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}
. Operationally, this means that we compare the results of the displacements after the astrometric reduction of the observed data to a common observer’s frame. An example would be the barycentric frame of the Solar System. Moreover, if we restrict ourselves to situations where only the observer (
\delta x^{\mathscr{E}}_{B}=0
) or only the emitter (
\delta x^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}=0
) has transverse variations, then we respectively obtain

(i)

Parallax of the redshift:

\displaystyle{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\ln\left(1+z\right)\equiv\delta x^{A^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\partial_{A^{\prime}}\ln(1+z)=

\displaystyle\qquad-\delta x^{A^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}\left(\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\,{\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}},{\bm{T}}\right)}\right).

(129)

Here we explicitly substitute
\bm{X}
and use the fact that
\ln(1+z)
is a bi–scalar. Meaning, the derivatives are taken with respect to both primed and unprimed coordinates.
This result can be viewed as the difference in the simultaneous redshift recordings of a point source by two observers: the fiducial observer and the one who is relatively displaced, but comoving with the first one. In analogy with the trigonometric parallax effect we call this effect the
parallax of the redshift
.

(ii)

Reversed parallax of redshift:

\displaystyle{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\ln\left(1+z\right)\equiv\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\,\partial_{B}\ln(1+z)=

\displaystyle\qquad-\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}\left(\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}{\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B}^{\mathscr{E}}},{\bm{T}}\right)}\right).

(130)

This quantity can be interpreted as follows: consider an extended source whose parts are all comoving. The difference of the redshift recording across the image of this extended source is then given by eq. (
130
). This result is obviously dual (reciprocal) to the parallax of the redshift, i.e., they are related by swapping the roles of
\mathscr{O}
and
\mathscr{E}
. Hence we propose the term
reversed parallax of the redshift
for it.

The parallax of redshift and the reversed parallax of redshift are not standard names in astrometry, but in the context of all possible variational observables they seem appropriate.

Transverse variations of positions

Now we consider the transverse variations of the position vector,
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}
. With transverse variations we refer to spatial changes with respect to the changes in the observer’s and the emitter’s worldlines.
Then, considering the definition of the position vector as in eq. (
107
) and taking the variation with respect to the bi–local vector given in eq. (
125
) results in

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle=\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\left(\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}+\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}\right)\,\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad=\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\delta x^{B^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}},{\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B}^{\mathscr{E}}},{\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}}\right).

(131)

We again assume that the observer’s 4–velocity does not change, i.e., the fiducial and the displaced observers are both co–moving.

Transverse variations of the viewing direction

Similar to the transverse variations of the position vector on the observer’s side, one can define the transverse variations of the viewing direction vector,
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}
, on the emitter’s side given in eq. (
109
). The bi–local variation vector is again represented by the one presented in eq. (
125
). That means we are considering the spatial, transverse variations of both the emitter and the observer. Assuming that the emitter’s 4–velocity
\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}
is covariantly constant, we have

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{A},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle=\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{A},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\left(\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}+\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}\,\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}\right)\,\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad=-\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}\delta x^{B^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}},{\bm{E}_{A}^{\mathscr{E}}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B}^{\mathscr{E}}},{\bm{E}_{A}^{\mathscr{E}}}\right).

(132)

Parallax matrix, magnifications and angular diameter distances

We now investigate how the apparent position on the observer’s sky changes with respect to his/her (i.e., the observer’s) own transverse, spatial variations. This corresponds to the well–known trigonometric parallax. On a curved background, the position on the sky is defined via the covariant changes of the position vector
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}
, i.e.,
{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}
. Now, we may also introduce orthogonal angular coordinates
\theta_{\mathscr{O}}^{A^{\prime}}
on the observer’s celestial sphere, centered at the unperturbed position
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}
, projected along transverse vectors
\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}
and measured in radians. Then, the variations of these coordinates
\delta\theta^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}
match exactly the components of
{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}r^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}
.

We now relate the covariant variation of the position vector to the variation of the null tangent
\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}
. First, note that
{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}
is equal to the derivative of the transverse component of the geodesic deviation vector with respect to the proper distance, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}r^{A^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}=\frac{d(\delta x^{A^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}})}{d\ell}=\frac{d(\delta x^{A^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}})}{\omega_{\mathscr{O}}d\lambda}=\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\nabla_{\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}}\delta x^{A^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}.

(133)

Here,
d\ell
is the infinitesimal proper length and its relation to the affine parameter,
\lambda
, and the observer’s proper time,
\tau^{\prime}
, follows as

\displaystyle d\ell=\omega_{\mathscr{O}}\,d\lambda=d\tau^{\prime},

(134)

in geometrized units with the speed of light
c=1
.
On the other hand, as we discussed in Section
II.3
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta l^{\mu}=\nabla_{\delta\vec{x}}l^{\mu}=\nabla_{\vec{l}}\,\delta x^{\mu}

(135)

in general due to the Lie dragging condition. This means the position variation can be defined through the covariant variation of the null tangent vector
\Delta\vec{l}
at
p_{\mathscr{O}}\,
, with
\Delta\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}\equiv{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}
in this case. Then, we write

\displaystyle\delta\theta^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}\equiv{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}r^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}=\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{\,A^{\prime}},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle.

(136)

Now, we are interested in only the observer’s transverse variations. Then, the bi–local variation vector is given by

\displaystyle\bm{X}=\delta x^{A^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}.

(137)

Substitution of the sub–matrices of the matrix
\mathbf{U}
and the components of
\bm{X}
as in the above gives

\displaystyle\delta\theta^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}\equiv{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}r^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}=-\mathchoice{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}\delta x^{B^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}.

(138)

with
\mathchoice{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}
being the parallax matrix, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}=-\frac{\partial\theta^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}}{\partial\delta x^{B^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}}=-\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}},{\bm{E}_{B^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}}\right).

(139)

The minus sign in the definition is chosen in order to be consistent with the definition of the trigonometric parallax in flat spacetime, i.e., the direction of the apparent position variation is
opposite
to the direction of the observer’s displacement.

One might also ask how the angles recorded at the observer changes with respect to the transverse variations at the emitter’s side. This is obviously related to the image distortions and the magnifications. In the standard gravitational lensing theory, the magnification and the distortion effects are defined with respect to their expected values in flat background. We can relate our results to the one of the standard lensing theory by considering the apparent position variations at linear order. The definition of magnification is similar to the parallaxes, however, this time we are interested in the variations of type

\displaystyle\bm{X}=\delta x_{\mathscr{E}}^{A}\,\bm{E}_{A}^{\mathscr{E}},

(140)

i.e., the one which involves transverse variations of the point source only,
rather than the variation in eq. (
137
).
We can again make use of eq. (
136
) for the variation of the apparent position, but with
\bm{X}
given by eq. (
140
).
The result can be written in the form

\displaystyle\delta\theta^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}\equiv{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}r^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}=\mathchoice{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}},

(141)

where

\displaystyle\mathchoice{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}=\frac{\partial\theta^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}}}{\partial\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}}=\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B}^{\mathscr{E}}},{\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}}\right),

(142)

is the
magnification matrix
. Note that in the standard lensing theory this matrix is usually rescaled by a transverse length scale.

The inverse of the magnification matrix tells us how the deviation vector at the emitter changes with respect to the angles recorded by the observer. In the literature, this matrix is known as the Jacobi matrix. In Section
II.5
we referred to the Jacobi matrix defined from the observer to the emitter as
\mathchoice{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{D}}\left(\lambda_{\mathscr{E}},\lambda_{\mathscr{O}}\right)
. As we discussed before, its determinant is a measure of the ratio of the cross sectional area of an extended object at the emission point to the solid angles recorded by the observer. In this respect, it gives the square of the angular diameter distance
D_{A}
up to a frequency factor, i.e.,

\displaystyle D_{A}^{2}=\left|{\rm{det}}\left({M}_{A^{\prime}B}^{-1}\right)\right|=\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{2}\,\left|{\rm{det}}\left[\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B}^{\mathscr{E}}},{\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}}\right)\right]\right|^{-1}.

Let us now compare the transverse variation of the position vector given in eq. (
131
) with the definitions of the parallax and the magnification matrices which are respectively given in eqs. (
139
) and (
142
).
For this, we rewrite eq. (
131
) as

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\delta x^{B^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}},{\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}}\right)

(144)

\displaystyle+\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B}^{\mathscr{E}}},{\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}}\right)

\displaystyle+\delta x^{B^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}\langle\,\vec{e}^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},\nabla_{B^{\prime}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle.

Then, we immediately realize that the transverse variation of the position vector is given through the difference of the effect of the magnification matrix,
\mathchoice{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}
, and the effect of the parallax matrix,
\mathchoice{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}
, in addition to the transverse variations of the 4–velocity of the observer, i.e.,

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathchoice{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}-\mathchoice{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}{\Pi^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}}}}\delta x^{B^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}

(145)

\displaystyle+\delta x^{B^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}\langle\,\vec{e}^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},\nabla_{B^{\prime}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle.

In the case where
\langle\,\vec{e}^{\mathscr{O}}_{A^{\prime}},\nabla_{B^{\prime}}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle=0
, i.e., the transverse variations of observer’s 4–velocity has no transverse components, the transverse variations of the position vector is precisely obtained through the effect of the parallax matrix and the magnification matrix. This is equivalent to assuming that we are comparing position measurements performed by displaced but strictly co–moving observers.

Reversed parallax matrix, reversed magnifications and luminosity distances

In the previous subsection, we investigated how the vector defining the position of the image on the observer’s sky changes with respect to the transverse variations of the observer or the emitter. Following a similar argument, one can investigate how the viewing direction of the emitter would change with respect to the transverse variations of the emitter or the observer. The viewing direction
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}
and its variation are defined in full analogy with the previous subsection, i.e., we have

\displaystyle{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}r_{\mathscr{E}}^{A}=\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}\nabla_{\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}}\delta x^{A}_{\mathscr{E}}.

(146)

We use the relationship between the derivative of the deviation vector
\nabla_{\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}}\delta x_{\mathscr{E}}^{A}
and the covariant variation of the null tangent vector
{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}\equiv\Delta\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}
, i.e., eq. (
135
), again. We then write the variation of emitter’s viewing direction as

\displaystyle{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}r^{\mathscr{E}}_{A}\equiv\delta\theta^{\mathscr{E}}_{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{A},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{l}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle.

(147)

Here, we define the angular coordinates
\theta_{\mathscr{E}}^{A}
related to the vectors
\vec{e}^{\mathscr{\,E}}_{A}
on the emitter’s celestial sphere, repeating the construction on the observer’s celestial sphere from the previous subsection.
Let us first investigate the trigonometric parallax effect on the emitter’s side, i.e., the variation of the viewing direction when the observer is fixed and the position of the emitter varies in the transverse direction. Then, the bi–local variation vector,
\bm{X}
, should be given by eq. (
140
) as we are interested in variations with respect to the emitter. Then, following the same procedure as in the previous section allows us to write the variation of the viewing direction in the form

\displaystyle\delta\theta^{\mathscr{E}}_{A}=-\mathchoice{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}.

(148)

with
\mathchoice{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}
being the parallax matrix of the emitter,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}=-\frac{\partial\theta^{\mathscr{E}}_{A}}{\partial\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}}=\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B}^{\mathscr{E}}},{\bm{E}_{A}^{\mathscr{E}}}\right).

(149)

We now demonstrate the analogue of the magnification matrix on the side of the emitter, i.e., how the viewing direction of the emitter changes with respect to the transverse variations of the observer. We refer to this effect as the
reversed image magnification and deformation
effect. The variation of emitter’s angles are defined as in eq. (
146
), however, now we consider the variation vector to be restricted only to the observer’s transverse plane. Thus, we substitute
\bm{X}
as in form in eq. (
137
) to eq. (
146
). Then, variation of the angles can be given in the form

\displaystyle\delta\theta_{A}=\mathchoice{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}\delta x^{B^{\prime}}.

(150)

with

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}=\frac{\partial\theta^{\mathscr{E}}_{A}}{\partial\delta x^{B^{\prime}}}=-\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}},{\bm{E}_{A}^{\mathscr{E}}}\right).

(151)

being the
reversed magnification matrix
.

Note that the inverse of
\mathchoice{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}
is also a Jacobi matrix. However this time it is the Jacobi matrix,
\mathchoice{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{D}}\left(\lambda_{\mathscr{O}},\lambda_{\mathscr{E}}\right)
, defined from the emitter to the observer. Its determinant incorporates how the cross sectional area of a collection of light rays at the observer’s location change with respect to the solid angles recorded by the emitter. This means, it is equivalent to the squared value of the luminosity distance, i.e.,

\displaystyle{D^{\prime}}_{L}{{}^{2}}=\left|{\rm{det}}\left({\tilde{M}}_{AB^{\prime}}^{-1}\right)\right|=\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{2}\,\left|{\rm{det}}\left(\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}},{\bm{E}_{A}^{\mathscr{E}}}\right)\right)\right|^{-1}.

Let us now return to the transverse variations of the viewing direction vector,
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}
, which was previously presented in eq. (
132
). We rewrite it as

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{\,A},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B}^{\mathscr{E}}},{\bm{E}_{A}^{\mathscr{E}}}\right)

(153)

\displaystyle-\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}\delta x^{B^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}\bm{{U}}\left({\bm{E}_{B^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}},{\bm{E}_{A}^{\mathscr{E}}}\right)

\displaystyle+\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{\,A},\nabla_{B}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle.

When we compare eqs. (
153
), (
149
) and (
151
) we observe that the transverse variation of the viewing direction vector is obtained through the differences of the effects of the reversed magnification matrix,
\mathchoice{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}
, and the reversed parallax matrix,
\mathchoice{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}
, in addition to the transverse variations of the 4–velocity of the emitter. In other words, we have

\displaystyle\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{\,A},{\bm{\nabla}}_{\bm{X}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}\delta x^{B^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{O}}-\mathchoice{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}

(154)

\displaystyle+\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{\,A},\nabla_{B}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle.

For the case where transverse variations of
\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}
have no component in the transverse direction,

\langle\,\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{E}}_{\,A},\nabla_{B}\vec{u}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle=0
, the viewing direction variation is given directly through the reversed magnification and reversed parallax matrices.

At this point, we exhausted all possible components of
\bm{U}
, considered as a quadratic form, restricted to the 7–dimensional subspace of the admissible vectors defined by (
21
). It turns out that the optical properties of the spacetime are related
only
to this restriction
[
59
]
, while the components of
\bm{U}
from outside that subspace are related to non–null geodesics, representing the propagation of massive fields or particles.

V
Physical outcomes

In the previous section, we derived variational observables by using the operator
\bm{{U}}\left({\cdot},{\cdot}\right)
. This operator can be viewed as a tool to construct scalar observables which result from bi–local variations at the emitter and at the observer. It is essentially defined through a symmetric block matrix
\mathbf{U}
which is the solution of the following boundary value problem: Given the position variation vectors at the both ends of a null geodesic, what is the resultant variation in its tangent vectors? In Sections
II.3
and
II.4
we showed that such a boundary value problem is equivalent to an initial value problem. In the equivalent form, the initial values of the position variation and the tangent vector variation defined at
p_{\mathscr{O}}\,
are linearly transformed to give the final values at
p_{\mathscr{E}}\,
. We showed that the corresponding linear transformation is indeed canonical and it is represented by a symplectic matrix
\mathbf{W}
.

In this section, we present various relationships in between the variational observables which might seem unrelated at a first glance. We show that those relationships follow from the underlying symplecticity of the first order dynamics. Equivalently, they manifest themselves through the symmetric property of matrix
\mathbf{U}
which is essentially the solution of the corresponding boundary value problem. In other words, operationally, the symplectic symmetries display themselves through the symmetric operator
\bm{{U}}\left({\cdot},{\cdot}\right)
which appears in all of the variational observables outlined in Section
IV.2
. Now, we investigate their relationships following from the symmetry of
\mathbf{U}
, or equivalently, from the symplectic property of
\mathbf{W}
.

V.1
Position drift–parallax of the redshift relationship

Let us compare the results we obtained for position drift in eq. (
IV.2
) and for parallax of the redshift in (
129
). Those are obtained from the operator
\bm{{U}}\left({\cdot},{\cdot}\right)
.
By making use of the symmetric property (
96
), it is straightforward to see that we have

\displaystyle\delta x_{\mathscr{O}}^{A^{\prime}}\,\,\partial_{A^{\prime}}\ln(1+z)=

\displaystyle\qquad\delta x_{\mathscr{O}}^{A^{\prime}}\left(\langle\,\vec{e}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}},\vec{a}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle-\langle\,\vec{e}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}},{\cal D}_{\tau^{\prime}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle\right).

(155)

Obviously, we discovered here an exact relation between the position drift on the right hand side and the parallax of the redshift on the left hand side. The former has the contribution from the aberration drift due to the non–gravitational acceleration subtracted. From this, we observe that for a free–falling, geodesic observer the parallax of the redshift and the position drift components are identical with opposite signs.

Figure 4
:

Sketch of a plane wave of constant frequency and slowly varying direction of propagation observed at two points
o_{1}
and
o_{2}
for two fixed moments of time.

This equality is fairly easy to understand in the case of a distant source moving in a flat spacetime.
Consider a plane wave of constant frequency
\omega
with a slowly varying direction of propagation,
\vec{n}
, due to the source’s motion (See Fig.
4
.).
Then the wave function has the form of

\displaystyle\Psi(t,\vec{x})=\exp\left(i\phi\right)=\exp\left(i\omega[t-\vec{n}(t)\cdot\vec{x}]\right),

(156)

the dot denoting the 3–dimensional, spatial vector product.
The phase
\phi
of the wave is measured by stationary observers at two points,
o_{1}
and
o_{2}
, which are respectively given by
\vec{x}=0
and
\vec{x}=\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{O}}
. The corresponding phases can be compared, for example, by the means of interferometry. The recorded phases are

\displaystyle\phi(t,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega\,t

(157)

\displaystyle\phi(t,\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{O}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega\,t-\omega\,\vec{n}(t)\cdot\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{O}}.

By differentiating these expressions with respect to
t
we obtain the frequencies of the wave measured at the two points as
\omega(t,0)=\omega
and

\omega(t,\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{O}})=\omega-\omega\,\dot{\vec{n}}(t)\cdot\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{O}}
. Obviously they differ by a small amount
\Delta\omega=-\omega\,\dot{\vec{n}}(t)\cdot\vec{\delta}x_{\mathscr{O}}
, related to the rate of change of the direction of propagation. Since the position on the observer’s sky can be identified with
-\vec{n}
and the observed frequencies are related to the redshifts as registered by both observers, it is straightforward to check that we have

\displaystyle\Delta\ln(1+z)=\dot{\vec{n}}(t)\cdot\vec{\delta}x_{\mathscr{O}}=-\langle\,\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{O}},{\cal D}_{t}\,\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}\,\rangle.

(159)

In other words, we re–derived eq. (
155
) for a non–accelerating observer in a flat spacetime this way.
In that case, the parallax of the redshift can be understood as the resultant effect of the slow drift of phases measured at two displaced points. The two phases drift away from each other due to the slow drift of the direction of wave propagation. This is in line with the result of the standard theory of interferometric measurements of positions of distant light sources in astrometry.

V.2
Viewing direction drift – reversed parallax of redshift relationship

A similar relationship can be demonstrated on the emitter’s side as well. Namely, this time we consider eqs. (
IV.2
) and (
130
) for viewing direction drift and the reversed parallax of redshift. We combine them with the help of the symmetry property in eq. (
96
) and the relation
1+z={\omega_{\mathscr{E}}}/{\omega_{\mathscr{O}}}
in order to obtain:

\displaystyle\delta x_{\mathscr{E}}^{B}\,\,\partial_{B}\ln(1+z)=

\displaystyle\qquad\delta x_{\mathscr{E}}^{B}\left((1+z)\,\langle\,\vec{e}_{\,B}^{\,\mathscr{E}},{\cal D}_{\tau^{\prime}}\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle-\langle\,\vec{e}_{\,B}^{\,\mathscr{E}},\vec{a}_{\mathscr{E}}\,\rangle\right).

(160)

This is a relation between the drift of the viewing angle as measured by the emitter and the variation of the redshift across the source’s image when the aberration drift contribution is absent. It is the exact reciprocal of relation (
155
). In fact, the reader may check that if we substitute the emitter’s proper time
\tau
for the observer’s proper time
\tau^{\prime}
in the viewing angle drift, (
160
), then the resulting expression will be the exact dual of (
155
), with a global sign difference.

Physically this relation means that for a perfectly static source, with no spin or non–gravitational acceleration whatsoever, any variation of the redshift accross the image of a source is precisely related to the variation of the viewing angle due to the motion of the source and the observer.

Variation of this kind would be extremely difficult to measure in astronomy, since the gradient of redshift across the source’s image is very small. Note that, just like the position drift–redshift parallax effect, this effect has fairly simple explanation in a flat spacetime: for a luminous body of finite size the direction of the line of sight varies slightly across the image, leading to a variation of the line–of–sight and transverse Doppler effects for a moving body. This in turn means that the redshift is slightly different in different parts of the image.

V.3
Parallax matrix and reverse parallax matrix are both symmetric

In Subsection
IV.2
we observed how the the transverse variations in the position and in the viewing direction vectors are related to their respective parallax and magnification matrices. In this subsection, we analyse physical outcomes of the symmetries of the parallax and the reversed parallax matrices themselves.

For this, let us re–write the parallax and the reversed parallax matrices which we previously presented in eqs. (
139
) and (
149
) respectively. This time, we present them explicitly by making use of the components of the
\mathbf{U}
matrix. They respectively follow as

We recall that
U^{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{O}}=U^{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{O}{\intercal}}
and
U^{\mathscr{E}\mathscr{E}}=U^{\mathscr{E}\mathscr{E}{\intercal}}
holds due the symmetry of
\mathbf{U}

(162)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B}}}
