\displaystyle-\frac{\partial\theta^{\mathscr{E}}_{A}}{\partial\delta x^{B}_{\mathscr{E}}}=-\frac{\partial\theta^{\mathscr{E}}_{B}}{\partial\delta x^{A}_{\mathscr{E}}}=\mathchoice{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A}}}{\tilde{\Pi}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A}}}.

(163)

We can interpret the equations above as the following. For this, we compare two situations. First, consider the variation of the observer’s position
\delta\vec{x}_{\mathscr{O}}
in one transverse direction, say
\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{\,1^{\prime}}
. This causes the geometric parallax effect in the form of the apparent displacement of the position of the image of a distant source in the direction of observer’s displacement. In a curved spacetime there might also be a perpendicular component of the displacement of the source’s image along
\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{\,2^{\prime}}
. Second, consider the variation of the observer’s position by the same distance in the other transverse direction, i.e.
\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{\,2^{\prime}}
. The apparent displacement of the source’s image in direction
\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{\,2^{\prime}}
is unrelated to the previous case, but the perpendicular displacement in direction
\vec{e}^{\,\mathscr{O}}_{\,1^{\prime}}
must be exactly the same as the transverse displacement in the previous case.
An analogous interpretation applies for the reversed situation as well, when we consider the celestial sphere of the emitter. This is implied by eq. (
163
).

V.4
Magnification matrix–Reversed magnification matrix relationship

Let us re–write the magnification matrix of the observer,
\mathchoice{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}
, and the reversed magnification matrix of the emitter,
\mathchoice{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A}{B^{\prime}}}}
, which were presented in eqs. (
142
) and (
151
) respectively. This time, we explicitly write them by using the the sub–blocks of
\mathbf{U}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathscr{O}}^{-1}\mathchoice{U^{{\mathscr{O}}{\mathscr{E}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\vec{e_{A^{\prime}}}}{\vec{e_{B}}}}}{U^{{\mathscr{O}}{\mathscr{E}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\vec{e_{A^{\prime}}}}{\vec{e_{B}}}}}{U^{{\mathscr{O}}{\mathscr{E}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displ \displaystyle\mathchoice{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A^{\prime}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\omega_{\mathscr{E}}^{-1}\mathchoice{U^{{\mathscr{E}}{\mathscr{O}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\vec{e_{B}}}{\vec{e_{A^{\prime}}}}}}{U^{{\mathscr{E}}{\mathscr{O}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\vec{e_{B}}}{\vec{e_{A^{\prime}}}}}}{U^{{\mathscr{E}}{\mathscr{O}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\vec{e_{B}}}{\vec{e_{A^{\prime}}}}}}{U^{{\mathscr{E}}{\mathscr{O}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.5382pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\vec{e_{B}}}{\vec{e_{A^{\prime}}}}}}.

(164)

Recall that we have
U^{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{E}}=U^{\mathscr{E}\mathscr{O}{\intercal}}
. Substitution of this symmetry allows us to relate the magnification and reversed magnification matrix via

\displaystyle\mathchoice{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}{M^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{A^{\prime}}{B}}}=-\left(1+z\right)\mathchoice{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A^{\prime}}}}.

(165)

This result can be viewed as the reciprocity of the magnification matrices. It is equivalent to the well-known Etherington’s reciprocity law for the Jacobi matrices
[
76
,
60
,
74
]
, as we will see in the next section.

V.5
Angular diameter distance–Luminosity distance relationship

We mentioned in Section
IV.2
that the inverse of the magnification matrix gives the Jacobi matrix,
\mathbf{D}\left(\lambda_{\mathscr{E}},\lambda_{\mathscr{O}}\right)
, defined from the observer to the emitter. Similarly, the inverse of the reversed magnification matrix is equal to the Jacobi matrix,
\mathbf{D}\left(\lambda_{\mathscr{O}},\lambda_{\mathscr{E}}\right)
, defined from the emitter to the observer. Those Jacobi matrices were first introduced in Section
II.5
in the context of cosmological distance calculations and their reciprocity.

Now, recall that the angular diameter distance is given in eq. (
IV.2
) and the luminosity distance is given in eq. (
IV.2
). They are obtained through the determinant of their respective Jacobi matrices. Then, if (i) we first take the inverse of the relation in eq. (
165
) that tells us how the magnification matrix is related to the reversed magnification matrix, (ii) then take the determinant of both sides, we obtain

\displaystyle{\rm{det}}\left[{M}_{A^{\prime}B}^{-1}\right]=\left(1+z\right)^{-2}{\rm{det}}\left[\mathchoice{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A^{\prime}}}}{\tilde{M}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{B}{A^{\prime}}}}^{-1}\right].

(166)

Note that the left hand side of the above gives the square of the angular diameter distance
D_{A}
and the right hand side involves the square of the corrected luminosity distance
D^{\prime}_{L}
, weighted by the
\left(1+z\right)^{-2}
term. This result is equivalent to

\displaystyle D^{\prime}_{L}=\left(1+z\right)\,D_{A},

(167)

and the distance reciprocity can be viewed as a simple consequence of the reciprocity of the magnification matrices represented in eq. (
165
).
Substituting the uncorrected, or bolometric luminosity distance
D_{L}=(1+z)\,D^{\prime}_{L}
yields the well–known distance duality relation,

\displaystyle D_{L}=\left(1+z\right)^{2}\,D_{A}.

(168)

VI
Summary and conclusions

In this work, we studied certain observables on the sky in the widest possible scope. In addition to the momentary observables such as the redshift, the position vector and the viewing direction vector; we considered variable observables such as the drift and parallax–like variations of those momentary observables. The investigation of such a large set of observables was conducted under a single, covariant, mathematical framework which was originally presented in a series of papers
[
27
,
52
,
53
,
59
]
. Some of its applications were presented in
[
53
,
77
]
.

In the current work, the aim was to unveil the underlying algebraic symmetries of variational observables. The reason for such an inquiry is not solely due to a mathematical concern but also due to our interest in the physical relations the symmetries provide. Namely, it is in virtue of the underlying symmetries that we were able to find the relationships between seemingly unrelated variational observables.

Let us now summarize the main layers of our construction.

(i)

We allow both the emitter,
\mathscr{E}
, and the observer,
\mathscr{O}
, to have arbitrary variations both in space and in time throughout the course of the measurement process.

(ii)

Accordingly, one has to abandon the
congruence
concept to study the propagation of collection of light rays. The space of congruences are at most 3–dimensional, while the space of all possible families of geodesics is 8–dimensional due to the need of two 4–dimensional tangent vectors at the end points of a geodesic. The dimension of this space reduces to 7 once we assume that the geodesic is null. This follows from the fact that both the coordinate positions,
\vec{x}
, and the tangent vectors of the null geodesics,
\vec{l}
, undergo variations in a scenario where the emitter and the observer perform variations of their own. Hence, defining a congruence around a fixed null geodesic and studying its evolution falls short for the identification of our problem.

(iii)

We study the light propagation problem on a symplectic phase space. We achieve this via studying the initial value problem of the covariant variations of positions,
\delta\vec{x}
, and covariant variations of the null tangent vectors,
\Delta\vec{l}
. Note that we are interested in only the first order part of the dynamics in this work. In addition, we consider the tetrad components of the phase space variables. Accordingly, the solution to the corresponding Hamiltonian initial value problem is given by a symplectic matrix,
\mathbf{W}
.

(iv)

Due to the Lie dragging condition,
\nabla_{\vec{l}}\,\delta\vec{x}=\nabla_{\delta\vec{x}}\vec{l}
, covariant variations of the null tangent vectors satisfy
\Delta\vec{l}=\nabla_{\vec{l}}\,\delta\vec{x}
. We use this fact to show that the matrix
\mathbf{W}
is also the solution matrix of the first order part of the null geodesic deviation equation. As physically meaningful quantities are obtained through the solutions of the geodesic deviation equation in relativity, the observables are obtained through
\mathbf{W}
. Essentially, the aforementioned algebraic symmetries of variational observables are indeed symplectic symmetries.

(v)

We then switch to a formulation which allows us to study the initial value problem mentioned in (iii) via an equivalent boundary value problem. Thereby, the symplectic matrix
\mathbf{W}
is interchangeable with the solution matrix,
\mathbf{U}
, of the corresponding boundary value problem. They are related to each other via an invertable transformation. It turns out that the symplectic symmetries of
\mathbf{W}
dictate
\mathbf{U}
to be a symmetric matrix and vice versa.

(vi)

In order to define the variational observables we first consider bi–local variation vectors. Those are 8–dimensional vectors which contain the information about two 4–dimensional variation vectors, one for
\mathscr{E}
and the other for
\mathscr{O}
, defined on distinct spacetime points. The endpoint variations of a null geodesic are not completely arbitrary though. Namely, in order for
\mathscr{E}
and
\mathscr{O}
to keep on receiving light–like signals while they perform variations, their position variation vectors have to satisfy
\langle\,\mathchoice{\vec{l}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{O}}}}{\vec{l}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{O}}}}{\vec{l}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{O}}}}{\vec{l}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{O}}}},\delta\mathchoice{\vec{x}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{O}}}}{\vec{x}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{O}}}}{\vec{x}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{O}}}}{\vec{x}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.13892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{O}}}}\,\rangle-\langle\,\mathchoice{\vec{l}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{E}}}}{\vec{l}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{E}}}}{\vec{l}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{E}}}}{\vec{l}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{E}}}},\delta\mathchoice{\vec{x}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{E}}}}{\vec{x}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{E}}}}{\vec{x}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{E}}}}{\vec{x}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.37502pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\mathscr{E}}}}\,\rangle=0
. We refer to any variation which satisfies this criteria as a
permissible
variation.

(vii)

Next, we construct variable observables via the solution matrix
\mathbf{U}
and the permissible bi–local variations. The matrix
\mathbf{U}
gives the values of
\Delta\vec{l}
at the emission and at the observation points which result from position variations,
\delta\vec{x}
, at the two end points of the null geodesics. Note that all of the variational observables listed in this work follow from the projection of
\Delta\vec{l}
on some vector which is also the solution of the geodesic deviation equation. Therefore, they all involve the sub–blocks of
\mathbf{U}
in their calculations. We list the variational observables that are constructed through the bi–local variations of the momentary observables in Table
1
.

(viii)

Finally, by using the fact that
\mathbf{U}
is a symmetric matrix, we construct relationships between the variable observables. The corresponding physical outcomes are explicitly given in Section
V
and they are listed in Table
2
along with the underlying symmetries. We should emphasise that the relationships between the variable observables are exact and valid in any spacetime for any source–observer pair as long as the geometric optics approximation works, i.e., the source is not passing through a caustic.

As we outlined in Section
I
, drift and parallax effects are instrumental for current cosmological studies. They provide important information about the inhomogeneity and anisotropy of our universe. Accordingly, drift and parallax effects are closely related to the latest challenges of the standard model such as the dark energy problem and the Hubble parameter tension.

Those problems are usually attacked with methods that involve data sets obtained from different types of measurements which are analysed separately. However, once equivalent data sets are considered simultaneously, interesting insights are obtained. For example, cosmological distance estimations are conducted either by (i) standard candles to obtain luminosity distances, or by (ii) standard rulers to obtain angular diameter distances. The reason we treat those two types of measurements in an equal footing is not trivial. It is due to the Etherington’s distance reciprocity that we treat them to be equal (up to a redshift factor). Previously, we showed that distance reciprocity follows from the symplectic symmetries of the light propagation on a reduced phase space
[
69
]
. Thus, different types of distance measurements can indeed be used interchangably due the symplectic symmetries of the null geodesic deviation dynamics.

In the current work, we enlarged the light propagation phase space and we studied variable emitters and observers. Studying the underlying algebraic symmetries of the light propagation allowed us to find analogous relationships between drift and parallax effects similar to the distance reciprocity. Hence, just as distance reciprocity sets certain constrains on cosmological models, the additional relationships we provide might be as useful in cosmological data analysis in the future. We believe this is a reasonable goal to achieve with ever improving accuracy in cosmological experiments. Our relations may also be of interest in the fields of precise astrometry or binary pulsar timing.

Table 1
:
Variational observables derived from bi–local variations of momentary observables. Those momentary observables are the logarithmic redshift,
\ln(1+z)
, the position vector,
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{O}}
, and the viewing direction vector,
\vec{r}_{\mathscr{E}}
. Variable observables are obtained through the bi–local variations of the momentary observables. For instance, the bi–local timelike variation,
\bm{T}
, of the position vector results in the position drift or the spatial transverse variation of the redshift with
\bm{E}_{A^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{O}}
gives the parallax of redshift. Swapping the last two rows and the last two columns corresponds to swapping the role of the observer and the source.

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathbf{W}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{X}{L}}}{\mathbf{W}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{X}{L}}}{\mathbf{W}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{X}{L}}}{\mathbf{W}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{X}{L}}}\mathchoice{\mathbf{W}^{{\intercal}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.52779pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.52779pt][c]{$\textstyle Table 2
:
Relationships between variational observables and the corresponding algebraic symmetries.

Appendix A
Proof that
\mathbf{W}_{XL}
is degenerate iff the Jacobi matrix
\mathbf{D}
is degenerate

For convenience we introduce an auxiliary, parallel-transported and semi-orthonormal vector tetrad
\left(\vec{f}_{\tilde{a}}\right)
, defined just like in Section
III.1
. It is given by
\left(\vec{u},\vec{l},\vec{e}_{A}\right)
, with
\vec{u}
being again a 4-velocity,
\vec{e}_{A}
denoting the transverse vectors and
\vec{l}
being the usual null tangent to the fiducial geodesic. The products of the basis vectors are given by eq. (
101
).

Its dual co–vector tetrad
(h^{\tilde{a}})
reads

\left(l^{\flat},\frac{u^{\flat}}{\omega}+\frac{l^{\flat}}{\omega^{2}},e_{A}^{\flat}\right)
, where
\flat
denote the index lowering operation using the spacetime metric
g
.

In
[
52
,
59
]
, a number of identities relating
\mathbf{W}
and the null vector
\vec{l}
were proven. In particular, it was shown that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{W}_{XL}}^{\tilde{a}\tilde{b}}\,l_{\mathscr{O}\,\tilde{b}}=\Delta\lambda\cdot\,{l_{\mathscr{E}}}^{\tilde{a}},\qquad l_{\mathscr{E}\,\tilde{a}}{\mathbf{W}_{XL}}^{\tilde{a}\tilde{b}}=\Delta\lambda\cdot{l_{\mathscr{O}}}^{\tilde{b}}.

By expressing these identities in the language of
(\vec{f}_{\tilde{a}})
and
(h^{\tilde{a}})
tetrads, we obtain

\mathbf{W}_{XL}\,\,h^{\tilde{0}}=\Delta\lambda\cdot f_{\tilde{1}}
and
h^{\tilde{0}}\circ\mathbf{W}_{XL}(\cdot)=\Delta\lambda\cdot f_{\tilde{1}}(\cdot)
. In the matrix notation this is in turn equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{XL}\,\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\\
0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\lambda\,\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
0\\
0\end{bmatrix},

(169)

\displaystyle\rm{and}

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{W}_{XL}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\lambda\,\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0&0\end{bmatrix}.

(170)

It follows that the matrix
\mathbf{W}_{XL}
needs to have the form of

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{XL}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{cc|ccc}0&\Delta\lambda&0&0\\
\Delta\lambda&*&*&*\\
\hline\cr 0&*&\lx@intercol\hfil\hbox{\multirowsetup$\mathbf{D}_{AB^{\prime}}$}\hfil\lx@intercol&\\
0&*&&&\\
\end{array}\right]

with
\mathbf{D}_{AB^{\prime}}
being the Jacobi matrix elements and “
*
” denoting an arbitrary value of the matrix element on the given slot.
We can now calculate the determinant of
\mathbf{W}_{XL}
with the help of the Laplace expansion with respect to the first row and then the first column:

\displaystyle\det{\mathbf{W}_{XL}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Delta\lambda^{2}\,\det\mathbf{D}.

(176)

It follows that
\det{\mathbf{W}_{XL}}=0
iff
\det\mathbf{D}=0
, i.e., the emitter sits on a caustic.
